The sequence in question involves an arithmetic progression where the third term is 24 and the fifth term is 384. To find the twelfth term, we first determine the common difference \( d \) and the initial term \( a \).

From the given terms:
- Third term (\( T_3 \)): \( a + 2d = 24 \)
- Fifth term (\( T_5 \)): \( a + 4d = 384 \)

Subtracting the first equation from the second gives:
\( (a + 4d) - (a + 2d) = 384 - 24 \)  
Which simplifies to:  
\( 2d = 360 \)  
So, \( d = 180 \).

Substituting \( d \) back into the first equation:
\( a + 2(180) = 24 \)  
\( a = 24 - 360 \)  
\( a = -336 \).

The nth term of an arithmetic sequence is given by:
\( T_n = a + (n-1)d \)

For the twelfth term (\( n = 12 \)):
\[
T_{12} = -336 + (12 - 1) \times 180  
= -336 + 11 \times 180  
= -336 + 1980  
= 1644
\]

The series is a geometric series with the first term \(a = 3\) and common ratio \(r = \frac{2}{3}\).

To find the sum of the first five terms (\(S_5\)):
\[ S_5 = a \cdot \frac{1 - r^5}{1 - r} = 3 \cdot \frac{1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^5}{1 - \frac{2}{3}} = \frac{211}{27} \]

The general formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms (\(S_n\)):
\[ S_n = 9 \cdot \left(1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n\right) \]

First, I need to understand the problem. We have one person who has two parents, four grandfathers (great-grandparents), eight great-great-grandfathers, and so on. The task is to find out how many ancestors this person has over ten generations.

Starting with the first generation:
- Generation 1: 2 parents

For each subsequent generation beyond the first, the number of ancestors doubles because each generation adds an additional set of grandparents:
- Generation 2: 4 grandparents
- Generation 3: 8 great-grandparents
- ...
- Generation 10: 512 great-great-great-great-great-great-grandfathers

To find the total number of ancestors over ten generations, I sum up all these values:

Total Ancestors = 2 + 4 + 8 + ... + 512

This is a geometric series where each term is double the previous one. The formula for the sum of a geometric series is:
Sum = first term × (ratio^n - 1) / (ratio - 1)

Here, the first term is 2, the ratio is 2, and n is 10.

Plugging in the numbers:
Sum = 2 × (2^10 - 1) / (2 - 1)
Sum = 2 × (1024 - 1) / 1
Sum = 2 × 1023
Sum = 2046

So, over ten generations, this person has a total of 2,046 ancestors.

To determine the total number of ancestors over ten generations for one person:

### Step-by-Step Solution:

**Given:**
- Generation 1 (Parents): 2
- Generation 2 (Grandparents): 4
- Generation 3 (Great-grandparents): 8
- ...
- Generation n (Great-(n-1)- grandparents): \( 2^n \)

We need to find the total number of ancestors over **ten generations**.

### Calculation:

Each generation contributes twice as many ancestors as the previous one. This forms a geometric series where each term is double the previous term.

The formula for the sum of a geometric series is:
\[
S_n = a_1 \times \frac{r^n - 1}{r - 1}
\]
Where:
- \( a_1 = 2 \) (first term)
- \( r = 2 \) (common ratio)
- \( n = 10 \) (number of terms)

Plugging in the values:
\[
S_{10} = 2 \times \frac{2^{10} - 1}{2 - 1} = 2 \times (1024 - 1) / 1 = 2 \times 1023 = 2046
\]

### Final Answer:

The total number of ancestors over ten generations is:

To solve the problem of inserting three numbers between 1 and 4096 such that the sequence forms a geometric progression (GP), follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Geometric Progression:**
   - In a GP, each term after the first is obtained by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the common ratio (\( r \)).
   - The sequence will look like: \( 1, a, b, c, 4096 \).

2. **Determine the Total Number of Terms:**
   - There are five terms in total (including both endpoints).
   - Therefore, the fifth term is given by:
     \[
     a_5 = 1 \times r^{4} = 4096
     \]

3. **Solve for the Common Ratio (\( r \)):**
   - From the equation \( r^4 = 4096 \), calculate \( r \):
     \[
     r = \sqrt[4]{4096} = 8
     \]

4. **Calculate the Inserted Numbers:**
   - Multiply by \( r \) successively to find each inserted number:
     \[
     a = 1 \times 8 = 8 \\
     b = 8 \times 8 = 64 \\
     c = 64 \times 8 = 512
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The three numbers to be inserted between 1 and 4096 are **8**, **64**, and **512**.

To find the 14th term (\(a_{14}\)) of a geometric progression (GP) where the 10th term (\(a_{10}\)) is 384 and the common ratio (\(r\)) is 4, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Formula for the nth Term of a GP
The general formula to find the \(n^{th}\) term of a GP is:
\[ a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)} \]
where:
- \(a_n\) is the \(n^{th}\) term,
- \(a_1\) is the first term,
- \(r\) is the common ratio.

### Step 2: Find the First Term (\(a_1\))
Given that the 10th term (\(a_{10} = 384\)) and the common ratio (\(r = 4\)), plug these values into the formula to find \(a_1\):
\[ a_{10} = a_1 \times r^{9} \]
\[ 384 = a_1 \times 4^{9} \]

Calculate \(4^9\):
\[ 4^9 = 262,144 \]

Now, solve for \(a_1\):
\[ a_1 = \frac{384}{262,144} = 0.001465 \] (approximately)

### Step 3: Calculate the 14th Term (\(a_{14}\))
Now that we have \(a_1\), use it to find the 14th term:
\[ a_{14} = a_1 \times r^{13} \]
\[ a_{14} = 0.001465 \times 4^{13} \]

Calculate \(4^{13}\):
\[ 4^{13} = 67,108,864 \]

Finally, multiply to find \(a_{14}\):
\[ a_{14} = 0.001465 \times 67,108,864 \approx 98,304 \]

### Final Answer
\[
\boxed{98,\!304}
\]

The problem involves finding the ninth term of a geometric sequence where the first term is -10, and the sixth term equals the square of the fourth term. 

Given:
- First term (a₁) = -10
- Sixth term (a₆) = (Fourth term)^2 → (-10r³)^2 = 100r⁶

Setting up the equation for a₆: 
(-10)r⁵ = 100r⁶  
Solving for r:
10r⁶ + r⁵ = 0  
r⁵(10r + 1) = 0 → r = -1/10

Calculating the ninth term (a₉):
a₉ = (-10) * (-1/10)^8  
= (-10) * (1/10^8)  
= -10 / 10,000,000  
= -1 / 1,000,000

The ninth term is \(-\frac{1}{1,000,000}\).

First, I need to identify whether the sequence is arithmetic or geometric based on the information provided. Since the user mentioned it's related to series and possibly involves sums being five times something, this suggests that we are dealing with a geometric progression.

In a geometric sequence, each term is multiplied by a common ratio r. Let me denote the first term as 'a' and the common ratio as 'r'. The sum of the first n terms of a geometric series can be expressed using the formula:

S_n = a * (1 - r^n) / (1 - r)

Given that the total sum is 5 times some value, I'll set up the equation accordingly. Let's assume S_n equals five times another term in the sequence for simplicity.

By solving this equation, I can find the common ratio 'r' which represents the required ratio between consecutive terms.
</think>

To solve the problem of finding the ratio in a geometric progression where the sum is given as 5x, we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Problem
We are dealing with a **geometric sequence**, which means each term after the first is found by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the **common ratio (r)**. The problem involves finding this common ratio based on the sum being five times another value.

### Step 2: Define the Terms
Let's denote:
- \( a \) as the first term of the geometric sequence.
- \( r \) as the common ratio we need to find.
- \( n \) as the number of terms in the sequence.
- \( S_n \) as the sum of the first \( n \) terms.

The sum of the first \( n \) terms of a geometric series is given by:
\[ S_n = a \times \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r} \]

### Step 3: Set Up the Equation
Given that the total sum \( S_n \) equals 5 times some value, let's assume it equals 5a for simplicity. Thus:
\[ S_n = 5a \]

Substituting the sum formula:
\[ a \times \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r} = 5a \]

### Step 4: Simplify the Equation
Divide both sides by \( a \) (assuming \( a \neq 0 \)):
\[ \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r} = 5 \]

Multiply both sides by \( 1 - r \):
\[ 1 - r^n = 5(1 - r) \]
\[ 1 - r^n = 5 - 5r \]

Rearrange the equation:
\[ -r^n + 5r - 4 = 0 \]

### Step 5: Solve for \( r \)
This equation can be challenging to solve directly, but assuming we're looking for a simple common ratio (e.g., \( n = 2 \)):
Let’s assume \( n = 2 \) for simplicity.

Substitute \( n = 2 \):
\[ -r^2 + 5r - 4 = 0 \]
Multiply both sides by -1:
\[ r^2 - 5r + 4 = 0 \]

Factor the quadratic equation:
\[ (r - 1)(r - 4) = 0 \]

So, \( r = 1 \) or \( r = 4 \).

Since a ratio of 1 would make it an arithmetic sequence rather than geometric, we choose \( r = 4 \).

### Final Answer
The common ratio is **4**.

First, I need to understand the problem. An individual deposits Rs. 10,000 in a bank that offers a simple interest rate of 5% per annum. The goal is to find out how much money will be available after 15 years and another 20 years.

To calculate the total amount after a certain number of years using simple interest, I use the formula:
A = P + (P × R × T)

Where:
- A is the final amount
- P is the principal amount (Rs. 10,000)
- R is the annual interest rate (5% or 0.05)
- T is the number of years

For the 15-year mark:
A = 10,000 + (10,000 × 0.05 × 15)  
First, calculate the simple interest: 10,000 × 0.05 = 500 per year; over 15 years, that's 500 × 15 = 7,500.

So, A = 10,000 + 7,500 = 17,500.

For the 20-year mark:
A = 10,000 + (10,000 × 0.05 × 20)  
Again, calculate the simple interest: 10,000 × 0.05 = 500 per year; over 20 years, that's 500 × 20 = 10,000.

So, A = 10,000 + 10,000 = 20,000.
</think>

To determine the total amount after a certain number of years using simple interest, we can use the formula:

\[ A = P + (P \times R \times T) \]

Where:
- \( A \) is the final amount
- \( P \) is the principal amount (Rs. 10,000)
- \( R \) is the annual interest rate (5% or 0.05)
- \( T \) is the number of years

### Calculation for 15 Years:
\[ A = 10,\!000 + (10,\!000 \times 0.05 \times 15) \]
First, calculate the simple interest:
\[ 10,\!000 \times 0.05 = 500 \text{ per year} \]
Over 15 years:
\[ 500 \times 15 = 7,\!500 \]

So,
\[ A = 10,\!000 + 7,\!500 = 17,\!500 \]

### Calculation for 20 Years:
\[ A = 10,\!000 + (10,\!000 \times 0.05 \times 20) \]
First, calculate the simple interest:
\[ 10,\!000 \times 0.05 = 500 \text{ per year} \]
Over 20 years:
\[ 500 \times 20 = 10,\!000 \]

So,
\[ A = 10,\!000 + 10,\!000 = 20,\!000 \]

First, I identify that this is an arithmetic progression (AP) because each term increases by a constant difference.

Next, I determine the common difference \( d \) by subtracting the first term from the second term:
\[ d = 9 - 4 = 5 \]

Then, I use the formula for the n-th term of an AP:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n-1)d \]
where \( a_1 = 4 \), \( d = 5 \), and \( n = 12 \).

Plugging in the values:
\[ a_{12} = 4 + (12-1) \times 5 = 4 + 55 = 59 \]

So, the twelfth term is 59.
</think>

**Solution:**

We are given an arithmetic progression (AP): **4, 9, 14, ...** and need to find the **12th term**.

An arithmetic progression follows the formula for the n-th term:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d \]
where:
- \( a_1 \) is the first term,
- \( d \) is the common difference,
- \( n \) is the term number.

**Step-by-step Solution:**

1. **Identify the First Term and Common Difference:**
   - First term (\( a_1 \)) = 4
   - Common difference (\( d \)) = 9 - 4 = 5

2. **Apply the Formula for the 12th Term:**
   \[
   a_{12} = a_1 + (n - 1)d \\
   a_{12} = 4 + (12 - 1) \times 5 \\
   a_{12} = 4 + 11 \times 5 \\
   a_{12} = 4 + 55 \\
   a_{12} = 59
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{59}
\]

**Step 1:** Identify the first term (\(a_1\)) and the common difference (\(d\)):
- First term (\(a_1\)) = 46
- Common difference (\(d\)) = -2

**Step 2:** The nth term of an arithmetic sequence is given by:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n-1)d \]

**Step 3:** Set the last term (\(a_n\)) to be equal to 4 and solve for \(n\):
\[
4 = 46 + (n - 1)(-2)
\]
\[
4 = 46 - 2(n - 1)
\]
\[
-42 = -2(n - 1)
\]
\[
21 = n - 1
\]
\[
n = 22
\]

**Final Answer:** The number of rows in the beehive is **22**.

First, I need to identify the type of sequence we're dealing with. The problem mentions an Arithmetic Mean (AM), so it's likely an arithmetic sequence.

Next, let's note down the given information:
- Total number of terms in the sequence (N) is 100.
- The sixth term (a6) is 24.
- The last term (a100) is 212.

I'll use the formula for the nth term of an arithmetic sequence: a_n = a1 + (n - 1)d, where a1 is the first term and d is the common difference.

Setting up two equations based on the given terms:
1. For the sixth term: a6 = a1 + 5d = 24
2. For the last term: a100 = a1 + 99d = 212

Now, I'll subtract the first equation from the second to eliminate a1 and solve for d:
(a1 + 99d) - (a1 + 5d) = 212 - 24
This simplifies to 94d = 188. Dividing both sides by 94 gives d = 2.

With the common difference found, I'll substitute it back into one of the original equations to find a1:
a1 + 5(2) = 24 → a1 + 10 = 24 → a1 = 14.

Finally, to find the 58th term (a58), I'll use the nth term formula again:
a58 = a1 + 57d = 14 + 57(2) = 14 + 114 = 128.
</think>

To solve the problem, we'll follow these steps:

**Given:**
- Total number of terms (\(N\)) = 100
- Sixth term (\(a_6\)) = 24
- Last term (\(a_{100}\)) = 212

**Objective:** Find the 58th term (\(a_{58}\)).

---

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Formula for Arithmetic Sequence:**
   The \(n^{th}\) term of an arithmetic sequence is given by:
   \[
   a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d
   \]
   where:
   - \(a_1\) = First term
   - \(d\) = Common difference

2. **Set Up Equations Based on Given Terms:**
   - For the sixth term (\(a_6\)):
     \[
     a_6 = a_1 + 5d = 24 \quad (1)
     \]
   - For the last term (\(a_{100}\)):
     \[
     a_{100} = a_1 + 99d = 212 \quad (2)
     \]

3. **Solve for Common Difference (\(d\)):**
   Subtract equation (1) from equation (2):
   \[
   (a_1 + 99d) - (a_1 + 5d) = 212 - 24
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   94d = 188 \\
   d = \frac{188}{94} = 2
   \]

4. **Find the First Term (\(a_1\)):**
   Substitute \(d = 2\) into equation (1):
   \[
   a_1 + 5(2) = 24 \\
   a_1 + 10 = 24 \\
   a_1 = 24 - 10 = 14
   \]

5. **Calculate the 58th Term (\(a_{58}\)):**
   Using the formula for \(a_n\):
   \[
   a_{58} = a_1 + 57d \\
   a_{58} = 14 + 57(2) \\
   a_{58} = 14 + 114 = 128
   \]

---

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{128}

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find the sum of the first 44 terms of an arithmetic progression. The given information is that the common difference D is 14 and the 44th term is 298.

First, let me recall some formulas related to arithmetic progressions (AP). In an AP, each term after the first is obtained by adding a constant called the common difference (D) to the preceding term. The nth term of an AP can be found using the formula:

a_n = a_1 + (n - 1) * D

Here, a_n is the nth term, a_1 is the first term, and D is the common difference.

I also know that the sum of the first n terms (S_n) of an AP is given by:

S_n = n/2 * [2a_1 + (n - 1)D]

Alternatively, it can also be written as:

S_n = n * (a_1 + a_n)/2

a_n = a_1 + (n - 1) * d

Here:
- \( a_n \) is the nth term we're interested in, which is 78.
- \( a_1 \) is the first term, which is 24.
- \( d \) is the common difference between consecutive terms, which is -3.

Simplifying this equation step by step will allow me to solve for n and determine how many terms are needed.
</think>

**Solution:**

We are given an arithmetic sequence with the first term \( a_1 = 24 \) and a common difference \( d = -3 \). We need to find how many terms (\( n \)) are required for one of the terms to be 78.

The general formula for the \( n^{th} \) term of an arithmetic sequence is:

\[
a_n = a_1 + (n - 1) \cdot d
\]

Plugging in the known values:

\[
78 = 24 + (n - 1) \cdot (-3)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Set up the equation:**
   \[
   78 = 24 + (n - 1)(-3)
   \]

2. **Subtract 24 from both sides to isolate the term with \( n \):**
   \[
   78 - 24 = (n - 1)(-3)
   \]
   \[
   54 = (n - 1)(-3)
   \]

3. **Divide both sides by -3 to solve for \( n - 1 \):**
   \[
   \frac{54}{-3} = n - 1
   \]
   \[
   -18 = n - 1
   \]

4. **Add 1 to both sides to find the value of \( n \):**
   \[
   -18 + 1 = n
   \]
   \[
   n = -17
   \]

Since a term count cannot be negative, this indicates that achieving a term with a value of 78 is not possible in this sequence.

**Answer:**

\boxed{\text{Necessary terms needed: Impossible}}

2. A * B = 64

I can set up a system of equations here. Let me let S = A + B and P = A*B. So, S=20 and P=64.

x = [20 ± sqrt( (20)^2 - 4*1*64 )]/2

Calculating discriminant D: 400 - 256 = 144. The square root of 144 is 12.

So, the solutions are:
x = [20 + 12]/2 = 32/2 = 16
and x = [20 - 12]/2 = 8/2 = 4

The quadratic equation with roots α = 16 and β = 6 can be formed using Vieta's formulas. 

- Sum of the roots (α + β) = 16 + 6 = 22
- Product of the roots (αβ) = 16 * 6 = 96

***********************<think>
First, I'll identify the given annual percentage growth rates for the business corporation from 2000 to 2005.

The provided data points are:
Year | Annual Growth Rate (%)  
--- | ---  
2000 | 50  
2001 | 72  
2002 | 54  
2003 | 82  
2004 | 93  

There are five growth rates over a six-year period, but since we're focusing on the years from 2000 to 2005, which includes 6 years (including both start and end), there are 5 intervals between these points. 

To calculate the geometric mean of these growth rates, I'll follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Product of All Growth Rates:** Multiply all the given growth rates together.
   
   Product = 50 × 72 × 54 × 82 × 93

2. **Determine the Number of Observations:** There are 5 growth rates.

3. **Compute the Geometric Mean:** Take the nth root (where n is the number of observations) of the product calculated in step 1.
   
   Geometric Mean = (Product)^(1/n)

By following these steps, I can determine the geometric mean growth rate that represents the average growth over the specified period.
</think>

**Solution:**

We are given the annual percentage growth rates for a business corporation from the year 2000 to 2005. The goal is to calculate the **Geometric Mean** of these growth rates.

The provided data points are:
\[
\begin{array}{|c|c|}
\hline
\text{Year} & \text{Annual Growth Rate (\%)} \\
\hline
2000 & 50 \\
2001 & 72 \\
2002 & 54 \\
2003 & 82 \\
2004 & 93 \\
\hline
\end{array}
\]

There are **five** growth rates over a period of five years (from 2000 to 2004, inclusive). However, since we're considering the growth from 2000 to 2005, which spans six years, there are **six data points**. To correctly calculate the geometric mean for these six years, we'll consider each year's growth rate as an annual increment.

\[
\text{Growth Rates} = [50,\ 72,\ 54,\ 82,\ 93]
\]

The formula to compute the **Geometric Mean (GM)** is:

\[
\text{GM} = \left( \prod_{i=1}^{n} x_i \right)^{\frac{1}{n}}
\]

Where:
- \( n \) = Number of observations
- \( x_i \) = Individual growth rates

**Step 1:** Calculate the product of all growth rates.

\[
50 \times 72 \times 54 \times 82 \times 93 = 16,066,560
\]

**Step 2:** Determine \( n \).

\[
n = 5
\]

**Step 3:** Compute the geometric mean.

\[
\text{GM} = \sqrt[5]{16,\!066,\!560} \approx 74.89\%
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Geometric Mean Growth Rate} \approx 74.89\%}
\]

***********************<think>
First, I calculate the initial population after two years of growth at 15% and 25%.

For the first year:
New population = Initial population * (1 + 15%)  
= 100,000 * 1.15  
= 115,000 people.

For the second year:
New population = Previous population * (1 + 25%)  
= 115,000 * 1.25  
= 143,750 people.

After two years, the population is 143,750.

In the third year, the population decreases by 5%:
Decrease amount = Current population * (1 - 5%)  
= 143,750 * 0.95  
= 136,562.5 people.

The average annual growth rate is calculated using the geometric mean formula:
Average growth rate = (Final population / Initial population)^(1/n) - 1

Here, n = 3 years.

Average growth rate = (136,562.5 / 100,000)^(1/3) - 1  
= 1.365625^(1/3) - 1  
≈ 0.11 or 11%.
</think>

To determine the average annual growth rate over three years with varying growth and decrease rates, follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate Population After Two Years of Growth**

- **Initial Population (Year 0):** 100,000
- **First Year Growth:** 15%  
  \[
  \text{Population after Year 1} = 100,000 \times (1 + 0.15) = 115,000
  \]
  
- **Second Year Growth:** 25%  
  \[
  \text{Population after Year 2} = 115,000 \times (1 + 0.25) = 143,750
  \]

**Step 2: Account for the Decrease in the Third Year**

- **Third Year Decrease:** 5%  
  \[
  \text{Population after Year 3} = 143,750 \times (1 - 0.05) = 136,562.5
  \]

**Step 3: Calculate the Average Annual Growth Rate**

The average annual growth rate (\(r\)) can be calculated using the geometric mean formula:

\[
\text{Average Growth Rate} = \left( \frac{\text{Final Population}}{\text{Initial Population}} \right)^{\frac{1}{n}} - 1
\]

Where:
- \( n = 3 \) years

Plugging in the values:

\[
r = \left( \frac{136,562.5}{100,000} \right)^{\frac{1}{3}} - 1 \\
r = (1.365625)^{\frac{1}{3}} - 1 \\
r \approx 1.11 - 1 \\
r \approx 0.11 \text{ or } 11\%
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Average Annual Growth Rate} : 11\%}
\]

9 = \frac{8 + 10 + K + 12}{4}
\]

Multiplying both sides by 4 to eliminate the denominator:
\[
36 = 30 + K
\]

Solving for \( K \):
\[
K = 36 - 30 = 6
\]
</think>

To determine the value of \( K \), we can use the concept of Arithmetic Mean (AM). The arithmetic mean is calculated by adding all the numbers together and then dividing by the count of numbers.

Given:
- **Numbers**: 8, 10, \( K \), 12
- **Arithmetic Mean (AM)**: 9

Let's follow these steps to find \( K \):

1. **Set up the equation using AM formula**:

   \[
   \text{AM} = \frac{\text{Sum of all numbers}}{\text{Total count of numbers}}
   \]

   Plugging in the known values:
   
   \[
   9 = \frac{8 + 10 + K + 12}{4}
   \]

2. **Multiply both sides by the total count to find the sum**:

   \[
   9 \times 4 = 30 + K
   \]
   
   \[
   36 = 30 + K
   \]

3. **Solve for \( K \)**:
   
   \[
   K = 36 - 30
   \]
   
   \[
   K = 6
   \]

**Final Answer**:  
\boxed{6}

First, I recognize that there are 14 numbers with an arithmetic mean of 6. This means the total sum of these numbers is 84.

</think>

**Solution:**

We are given that there are **14 numbers** with an **arithmetic mean (AM)** of **6**.

\[
\text{Total sum} = \text{Number of terms} \times \text{Arithmetic Mean} = 14 \times 6 = 84
\]

If we add three more numbers, each equal to the original AM (i.e., **6**), then:

- Total number of terms becomes \(14 + 3 = 17\)  
- Total sum becomes \(84 + (3 \times 6) = 84 + 18 = 102\)

Now, calculate the new Arithmetic Mean:

\[
\text{New AM} = \frac{\text{Total sum after adding three numbers}}{\text{New number of terms}} = \frac{102}{17} = 6
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{6}
\]

Given a function \( f \) such that for all \( n \geq 1 \), \( f(n) = 3n + 2 \). Let \( S \) be the set of positive integers \( n \) satisfying \( f(n) = f(m) + f(k) \) for some \( m, k \) in \( S \). We need to find the number of elements in \( S \).

First, we express the condition given by the function:
\[ f(n) = 3n + 2 \]
If \( n \in S \), there must exist \( m, k \in S \) such that:
\[ f(n) = f(m) + f(k) \]
Substituting the function into this equation, we get:
\[ 3n + 2 = (3m + 2) + (3k + 2) \]
Simplifying the right-hand side:
\[ 3n + 2 = 3(m + k) + 4 \]
Rearranging terms, we have:
\[ 3n + 2 - 4 = 3(m + k) \]
\[ 3n - 2 = 3(m + k) \]
Dividing both sides by 3:
\[ n - \frac{2}{3} = m + k \]

Since \( m \) and \( k \) are positive integers, their sum \( m + k \) is also a positive integer. However, the left-hand side \( n - \frac{2}{3} \) is not an integer because it results in a non-integer value when subtracted by \( \frac{2}{3} \).

This implies that there are no positive integers \( n, m, k \) such that the equation holds. Therefore, the set \( S \) must be empty.

Thus, the number of elements in \( S \) is:
\[
\boxed{0}
\]

First, I recognize that this is a linear recurrence relation of the form \( a_n = 3a_{n-1} + 2 \) with the initial condition \( a_1 = 3 \). 

To solve such equations, it's helpful to find both the homogeneous solution and a particular solution. The homogeneous part corresponds to \( a_n - 3a_{n-1} = 0 \), which has the characteristic equation \( r - 3 = 0 \) leading to \( r = 3 \). Thus, the homogeneous solution is \( A \cdot 3^n \).

Next, I look for a particular solution. Since the nonhomogeneous term is a constant (2), I assume a constant particular solution of the form \( B \). Substituting into the recurrence relation:

\[
B = 3B + 2
\]

Solving for \( B \) gives:

\[
-2B = 2 \\
B = -1
\]

Therefore, the general solution is the sum of the homogeneous and particular solutions:

\[
a_n = A \cdot 3^n - 1
\]

Applying the initial condition \( a_1 = 3 \):

\[
3 = A \cdot 3^1 - 1 \\
A = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Finally, substituting \( A \) back into the general solution yields:

\[
a_n = \left( \frac{4}{3} \right) \cdot 3^n - 1 = 4 \cdot 3^{n-1} - 1
\]
</think>

To determine the explicit formula for the sequence defined by:
\[
A_1 = 3, \quad A_n = 3A_{n-1} + 2 \quad \text{for all } n > 1,
\]
we follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Homogeneous Solution
The homogeneous part of the recurrence relation is:
\[
A_n^{(h)} = 3A_{n-1}^{(h)}
\]
This has the characteristic equation \( r - 3 = 0 \), giving a root \( r = 3 \). Thus, the homogeneous solution is:
\[
A_n^{(h)} = A \cdot 3^n
\]

### Step 2: Find a Particular Solution
Assume a particular solution of the form \( A_n^{(p)} = B \) (a constant), since the nonhomogeneous term is constant.

Substitute into the recurrence relation:
\[
B = 3B + 2 \\
-2B = 2 \\
B = -1
\]

### Step 3: Combine Homogeneous and Particular Solutions
The general solution combines both parts:
\[
A_n = A \cdot 3^n - 1
\]

### Step 4: Apply the Initial Condition
Use \( A_1 = 3 \) to solve for \( A \):
\[
3 = A \cdot 3^1 - 1 \\
3 + 1 = 3A \\
A = \frac{4}{3}
\]

### Final Explicit Formula
Substituting \( A \) back into the general solution:
\[
A_n = \left( \frac{4}{3} \right) \cdot 3^n - 1 = 4 \cdot 3^{n-1} - 1
\]

This formula gives the nth term of the sequence.

     \]

     \[

     \]

The sum of the first five terms of the geometric progression with initial term 3 and common ratio 2/3 is calculated using the formula for the sum of a geometric series, resulting in approximately 7.8148.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Identify the given values:
   - First term (a) = 3
   - Common ratio (r) = 2/3
   - Number of terms (n) = 5

2. Use the formula for the sum of the first n terms of a geometric series:
   \[
   S_n = a \times \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r}
   \]

3. Plug in the values into the formula:
   \[
   S_5 = 3 \times \frac{1 - (2/3)^5}{1 - (2/3)}
   \]

4. Calculate \( (2/3)^5 = 32/243 \).

5. Compute the numerator: \( 1 - 32/243 = 211/243 \).

6. The denominator is \( 1 - 2/3 = 1/3 \).

7. Substitute back into the formula:
   \[
   S_5 = 3 \times \frac{211/243}{1/3} = 3 \times (211/243) \times 3
   \]

8. Simplify: \( 3 × 3 = 9 \), so:
   \[
   S_5 = \frac{9 × 211}{243}
   \]

9. Simplify the fraction by dividing numerator and denominator by 9 (since 243 ÷9=27, 1899 ÷9=211):
   \[
   S_5 = \frac{211}{27} ≈ 7.8148
   \]

Answer: The sum of the first five terms is approximately 7.8148.

$\boxed{\dfrac{211}{27}}$

To determine if there exists an integer \( n \) such that the \( n \)-th term of the geometric sequence equals \( \frac{3^{6141}}{1024} \), we start by expressing the general term of the sequence.

Given:
- First term (\( a_1 \)) = 3
- Common ratio (\( r \)) = \( \frac{1}{2} \)

The \( n \)-th term of the geometric sequence is given by:
\[ a_n = a_1 \cdot r^{n - 1} \]
Substituting the known values:
\[ a_n = 3 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{n - 1} \]

We set this equal to \( \frac{3^{6141}}{1024} \):
\[ 3 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{n - 1} = \frac{3^{6141}}{1024} \]

Divide both sides by 3:
\[ \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{n - 1} = \frac{3^{6140}}{1024} \]

Taking the natural logarithm of both sides:
\[ (n - 1) \cdot \ln\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \ln(3^{6140}) - \ln(1024) \]
\[ -(n - 1) \cdot \ln(2) = 6140 \cdot \ln(3) - 10 \cdot \ln(2) \]

Solving for \( n \):
\[ (n - 1) = \frac{10 \cdot \ln(2) - 6140 \cdot \ln(3)}{\ln(2)} \]
Since the numerator is negative, this implies that no positive integer \( n \) satisfies the equation.

**Answer:**

No such term exists in the sequence. Therefore, there is no integer \( n \) for which the \( n \)-th term equals \( \frac{3^{6141}}{1024} \).

The problem asks for the first three terms of a harmonic progression (H.P.) where the arithmetic mean (A.M.) is given as \( \frac{13}{12} \).

In a harmonic progression, if all terms are equal, their reciprocals form an arithmetic progression with common difference zero. Let each term be \( t \). The arithmetic mean of these three terms is:

\[
\frac{t + t + t}{3} = t
\]

Given that the A.M. is \( \frac{13}{12} \), we have:

\[
t = \frac{13}{12}
\]

Thus, each term in the H.P., including the first three terms, is \( \frac{13}{12} \).

**Answer:** The first three terms are all \( \boxed{\dfrac{13}{12}} \).

The problem involves determining the number of children an individual has across ten generations. 

First, I need to understand the given data:
- The person has 2 parents.
- They have 4 grandparents.
- There are 8 great-grandparents.

This indicates that each generation beyond the first doubles in terms of ancestors. 

To calculate the total number of children (and thus the number of individuals across all generations), I'll sum up the number of descendants at each level from generation 1 to generation 10.

By adding these numbers together, I arrive at the total count.
</think>

**Solution:**

We are tasked with determining the total number of children an individual has over ten generations. Here's how we can approach this problem step-by-step:

### Given:
- **Generation 1 (Individual):** 1 person
- **Generation 2 (Parents):** 2 children
- **Generation 3 (Grandparents):** 4 grandchildren
- **Generation 4 (Great-grandparents):** 8 great-great-grandchildren

From the pattern observed, each subsequent generation doubles in number. This follows a geometric progression where the number of people at each level is \( 2^{n-1} \), with \( n \) being the generation number.

### Calculations:

We will calculate the number of individuals for each generation from 1 to 10 and then sum them up.

\[
\begin{align*}
\text{Generation } 1 & : 2^{1-1} = 2^0 = 1 \\
\text{Generation } 2 & : 2^{2-1} = 2^1 = 2 \\
\text{Generation } 3 & : 2^{3-1} = 2^2 = 4 \\
\text{Generation } 4 & : 2^{4-1} = 2^3 = 8 \\
\text{Generation } 5 & : 2^{5-1} = 2^4 = 16 \\
\text{Generation } 6 & : 2^{6-1} = 2^5 = 32 \\
\text{Generation } 7 & : 2^{7-1} = 2^6 = 64 \\
\text{Generation } 8 & : 2^{8-1} = 2^7 = 128 \\
\text{Generation } 9 & : 2^{9-1} = 2^8 = 256 \\
\text{Generation } 10 & : 2^{10-1} = 2^9 = 512 \\
\end{align*}
\]

### Summing Up All Generations:

To find the total number of people across all ten generations, we sum up the individuals from each generation:

\[
\text{Total} = \sum_{n=1}^{10} 2^{n-1} = 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 + 16 + 32 + 64 + 128 + 256 + 512
\]

\[
\text{Total} = 1023
\]

### Final Answer:

The total number of children (and thus the number of individuals across all ten generations) is **1023**.

```json
{'Final Answer': '1023'}
```

Alright, so I need to figure out how to insert three numbers between 1 and 4096 such that the resulting sequence is a geometric progression (GP). Let me break this down step by step.

First off, what's a GP? It stands for Geometric Progression. In a GP, each term after the first is found by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the common ratio, denoted as 'r'. So if I have terms like a1, a2, a3,... an in a GP, then:

- a2 = a1 * r
- a3 = a2 * r = a1 * r²
- And so on.

The problem says to insert three numbers between 1 and 4096. That means the series will have five terms in total: the first term is 1, then three inserted numbers, and the fifth term is 4096.

Let me denote the terms as follows:
- Term 1 (a₁): 1
- Term 2 (a₂): The first inserted number
- Term 3 (a₃): The second inserted number
- Term 4 (a₄): The third inserted number
- Term 5 (a₅): 4096

Since it's a GP, I can express each term in terms of the first term and the common ratio 'r'.

So,
- a₁ = 1
- a₂ = a₁ * r = r
- a₃ = a₂ * r = r²
- a₄ = a₃ * r = r³
- a₅ = a₄ * r = r⁴

But we know that a₅ is given as 4096. So:

a₅ = 1 * r⁴ = r⁴ = 4096

To find 'r', I can take the fourth root of both sides:

r⁴ = 4096
r = √[4]{4096}

I know that 4096 is a power of 2. Let me check: 2^12 = 4096, because 2^10=1024 and 2^12=4096.

So,

r⁴ = 2¹²

Taking the fourth root:

Okay, so the common ratio 'r' is 8.

Now I can find each of the inserted terms:

- a₂ = r = 8
- a₃ = r² = 8² = 64
- a₄ = r³ = 8³ = 512

a₅ = 512*8 = 4096

So everything checks out.

Given a geometric progression (GP) with the first term \(a = 1\) and common ratio \(r = 4\), we need to find the number of terms \(n\) such that the sum of the series equals 63.

The formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms of a GP is:
\[ S_n = \frac{a(r^n - 1)}{r - 1} \]

Substituting the given values \(a = 1\) and \(r = 4\):
\[ S_n = \frac{4^n - 1}{3} \]

We need this sum to be equal to 63:
\[ \frac{4^n - 1}{3} = 63 \]
\[ 4^n - 1 = 189 \]
\[ 4^n = 190 \]

Taking the natural logarithm on both sides:
\[ n \ln(4) = \ln(190) \]
\[ n = \frac{\ln(190)}{\ln(4)} \approx \frac{5.25}{1.386} \approx 3.78 \]

Since \(n\) must be an integer, we check the sums for integer values of \(n\):
- For \(n = 3\), the sum is 21.
- For \(n = 4\), the sum is 85.

The sum exceeds 63 at \(n = 4\). Therefore, the closest answer is:

\[
\boxed{4}
\]

First, I need to understand the problem presented. It mentions an Arithmetic Mean-Geometric Mean (AM-GM) inequality scenario involving sequences or series, specifically referring to a geometric progression where the sum of certain terms equals 5 times another set of terms.

I'll denote the first term as \(a\) and the common ratio as \(r\). The problem states that the sum of some number of terms is five times another sum. Without loss of generality, let's assume we're dealing with four terms in each case: one group where the sum is 5 times the other.

Using the formula for the sum of a geometric series:

\(S = a \frac{r^4 - 1}{r - 1}\)  
and  
\(S' = a r^2 \frac{r^4 - 1}{r - 1}\)

According to the problem, \(S' = 5S\). Substituting the expressions:

\(a r^2 \frac{r^4 - 1}{r - 1} = 5 a \frac{r^4 - 1}{r - 1}\)

Simplifying by canceling common terms:

\(r^2 = 5\)

Taking the square root of both sides gives \(r = \sqrt{5}\).

Finally, to find the common ratio in its simplest radical form:  
\(r = \sqrt{5}\)
</think>

To solve the problem using the Arithmetic Mean-Geometric Mean (AM-GM) inequality and geometric progression concepts:

**Given:**
- A geometric sequence where the sum of certain terms is five times another set of terms.
- Specifically, consider four terms in each case.

Let's denote:
- First term = \( a \)
- Common ratio = \( r \)

**Sum of Four Terms:**
\[
S = a + ar + ar^2 + ar^3
\]
\[
= a (1 + r + r^2 + r^3) 
\]

**Sum of Next Four Terms:**
These terms are multiplied by \( r^4 \):
\[
S' = ar^4 + ar^5 + ar^6 + ar^7
\]
\[
= ar^4 (1 + r + r^2 + r^3)
\]

According to the problem, \( S' = 5S \):
\[
ar^4 (1 + r + r^2 + r^3) = 5 a (1 + r + r^2 + r^3)
\]
\[
r^4 = 5
\]
\[
r = \sqrt[4]{5}
\]

To solve this problem, I need to determine the number of bacteria present after a certain number of minutes, given that their count increases exponentially.

I know that exponential growth can be modeled using a geometric progression. The general formula for exponential growth is:

\[ N(t) = N_0 \times r^t \]

Where:
- \( N(t) \) is the amount at time \( t \),
- \( N_0 \) is the initial amount,
- \( r \) is the growth factor per period (in this case, per minute),
- \( t \) is the number of periods.

Given:
- The bacteria count triples every minute (\( r = 3 \)),
- Initial count (\( N_0 \)) is 50,
- Time elapsed (\( t \)) is 10 minutes.

I can substitute these values into the formula to find \( N(10) \):

\[ N(10) = 50 \times 3^{10} \]

Now, I'll calculate the numerical value:

First, compute \( 3^{10} \):
\[ 3^{10} = 59049 \]

Then multiply by 50:
\[ N(10) = 50 \times 59049 = 2952450 \]

So, after 10 minutes, there will be **2,952,450** bacteria present.

</think>

To determine the total price of the scooter, we'll break down the payments Shamsad Ali makes over time.

1. **Initial Price**: The price of the scooter is **Rs. 44,000**.

2. **Down Payment**: Shamsad Ali paid **Rs. 8,000** in cash.

3. **Remaining Balance**: After the down payment, the remaining balance is:
   \[
   44,000 - 8,000 = Rs. \ 36,000
   \]

4. **Annual Payments**: Each year, Shamsad Ali pays **Rs. 2,000** plus **20% interest** on the remaining balance.

5. **Geometric Progression**: The payments form a geometric sequence where each term is multiplied by a constant ratio (in this case, 80%, since he pays 20% of the remaining balance as interest and principal).

6. **Total Price Calculation**:
   - Total price = Down payment + Sum of all annual payments
   - Since it's an infinite geometric series with first term \(a = Rs. \ 36,000\) and common ratio \(r = 0.8\), the sum is:
     \[
     S = \frac{a}{1 - r} = \frac{36,000}{1 - 0.8} = \frac{36,000}{0.2} = Rs. \ 180,000
     \]
   - Adding the down payment:
     \[
     Total\ Price = 8,000 + 180,000 = Rs. \ 188,000
     \]

\[ 4(n - 1) = 46 \]

**Final Answer**
The integral evaluates to \boxed{\frac{1}{2} \arcsin t + \frac{t\sqrt{1 - t^2}}{2} + C}.
</think>

To evaluate the integral involving $\sqrt{1 - t^2}$, we can use a trigonometric substitution. Here are the key steps:

1. **Substitution**: Let $t = \sin \theta$. Then, $dt = \cos \theta \, d\theta$.
2. **Simplify the integrand**: The term $\sqrt{1 - t^2}$ becomes $\sqrt{1 - \sin^2 \theta} = \cos \theta$.
3. **Rewrite the integral**: Substituting $t$ and $dt$, the integral becomes:
   \[
   \int \sqrt{1 - t^2} \, dt = \int \cos \theta \cdot \cos \theta \, d\theta = \int \cos^2 \theta \, d\theta
   \]
4. **Use trigonometric identity**: Apply the power-reduction formula $\cos^2 \theta = \frac{1 + \cos 2\theta}{2}$:
   \[
   \int \cos^2 \theta \, d\theta = \int \frac{1 + \cos 2\theta}{2} \, d\theta
   \]
5. **Integrate term by term**:
   \[
   \int \frac{1 + \cos 2\theta}{2} \, d\theta = \frac{1}{2} \int 1 \, d\theta + \frac{1}{2} \int \cos 2\theta \, d\theta
   \]
6. **Evaluate the integrals**:
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \int 1 \, d\theta = \frac{1}{2} \theta
   \]
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \int \cos 2\theta \, d\theta = \frac{1}{4} \sin 2\theta
   \]
7. **Combine the results**:
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \theta + \frac{1}{4} \sin 2\theta + C
   \]
8. **Revert back to $t$**: Since $t = \sin \theta$, we have $\theta = \arcsin t$. Also, $\sin 2\theta = 2 \sin \theta \cos \theta = 2t \sqrt{1 - t^2}$.
9. **Final expression**:
   \[
   \frac{1}{2} \arcsin t + \frac{1}{4} (2t \sqrt{1 - t^2}) + C = \frac{1}{2} \arcsin t + \frac{t \sqrt{1 - t^2}}{2} + C
   \]

Thus, the integral evaluates to:

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{2} \arcsin t + \frac{t\sqrt{1 - t^2}}{2} + C}
\]

- The difference between target and starting income is 14,000 - 10,000 = Rs.4,000.

The problem involves verifying that the ratio of the sum of two numbers to their geometric mean is 12. The numbers are given as \(6 + \sqrt{35}\) and \(6 - \sqrt{35}\).

1. **Calculate the Sum (a + b):**
   \[
   a = 6 + \sqrt{35}, \quad b = 6 - \sqrt{35}
   \]
   \[
   a + b = (6 + \sqrt{35}) + (6 - \sqrt{35}) = 12
   \]

2. **Calculate the Geometric Mean (GM):**
   The geometric mean is given by:
   \[
   GM = \sqrt{ab}
   \]
   First, calculate \( ab \):
   \[
   ab = (6 + \sqrt{35})(6 - \sqrt{35}) = 6^2 - (\sqrt{35})^2 = 36 - 35 = 1
   \]
   Then, take the square root:
   \[
   GM = \sqrt{1} = 1
   \]

3. **Verify the Ratio:**
   \[
   \frac{a + b}{GM} = \frac{12}{1} = 12
   \]
   
The ratio is indeed 12, as required.

To determine the average growth rate over three years, we need to calculate the geometric mean because it accounts for compounding effects.

First, convert each percentage increase or decrease into its corresponding growth factor:
- 15% increase = 1 + 0.15 = 1.15
- 25% increase = 1 + 0.25 = 1.25
- 5% decrease = 1 - 0.05 = 0.95

Next, multiply these growth factors together to find the overall growth over three years:
1.15 * 1.25 * 0.95 = 1.34375

Then, take the cube root (since there are three periods) of this product to find the geometric mean growth factor per period:
³√1.34375 ≈ 1.10

Finally, convert this back to a percentage by subtracting 1 and multiplying by 100:
(1.10 - 1) * 100 = 10%

The average annual growth rate is approximately 10%.
</think>

To find the **average growth rate** over three years with growth rates of +15%, +25%, and -5%, we use the **geometric mean**, as it accounts for compounding effects.

### Steps:

1. **Convert Growth Rates to Decimal Multipliers:**
   - 15% increase = \( 1 + \frac{15}{100} = 1.15 \)
   - 25% increase = \( 1 + \frac{25}{100} = 1.25 \)
   - 5% decrease = \( 1 - \frac{5}{100} = 0.95 \)

2. **Calculate the Product of Multipliers:**
   \[
   1.15 \times 1.25 \times 0.95 = 1.34375
   \]

3. **Determine the Geometric Mean:**
   - Since there are three periods, take the cube root (3rd root) of the product:
   \[
   \sqrt[3]{1.34375} \approx 1.10
   \]
   
4. **Convert Back to Percentage:**
   \[
   1.10 - 1 = 0.10 \quad \text{or} \quad 10\%
   \]

### Final Answer:
\boxed{\text{Final Answer} : 10\%}

**Final Answer**
\boxed{11}
</think>

The given sequence of numbers is 9, 8, 10, \(k\), 12. The task is to determine the value of \(k\).

After considering various approaches such as arithmetic sequences, letter mappings, and element periodicity, it was found that mapping these numbers to elements in the periodic table provides a plausible solution. Specifically:

- 9 corresponds to Helium (He),

### Step 1: Understand the Arithmetic Progression Formula
The general formula for the \( n^{th} \) term of an A.P. is:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d \]
Where:
- \( a_n \) is the \( n^{th} \) term.
- \( a_1 \) is the first term.
- \( d \) is the common difference between terms.

- First term (\( a_1 \)) = \( 2N + 5 \)

First, I recognize that this sequence follows a recurrence relation where each term is defined based on the previous term. The given initial condition is A₁ = 3, and the recursive formula for n > 1 is Aₙ = 3Aₙ₋₁ + 2.

To solve this linear nonhomogeneous recurrence relation, I'll first find the general solution to the homogeneous equation associated with it. The homogeneous version of the recurrence relation is:

Aₙ - 3Aₙ₋₁ = 0

The characteristic equation for this homogeneous recurrence is r - 3 = 0, which gives a root of r = 3. Therefore, the general solution to the homogeneous equation is:

Aₙ^h = C * 3ⁿ

Next, I'll find a particular solution to the nonhomogeneous recurrence relation. Since the nonhomogeneous term is a constant (2), I'll assume that the particular solution is also a constant, say Aₙ^p = K.

Substituting this into the original recurrence relation:

K = 3K + 2

Solving for K gives K - 3K = 2 → -2K = 2 → K = -1

So, the general solution to the nonhomogeneous recurrence is the sum of the homogeneous and particular solutions:

Aₙ = C * 3ⁿ - 1

Now, applying the initial condition A₁ = 3:

3 = C * 3¹ - 1 → 3 + 1 = 3C → C = 4/3

Finally, substituting back into the general solution gives the explicit formula for the sequence:

Aₙ = (4/3) * 3ⁿ - 1

**Solution:**

We are given a recursive sequence defined by:
- Initial term: \( A_1 = 3 \)
- Recursive relation: \( A_n = 3A_{n-1} + 2 \) for all \( n > 1 \)

To find an explicit formula for the general term \( A_n \), we can solve this linear nonhomogeneous recurrence relation.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Find the Homogeneous Solution (\( A_n^h \)):**

   The homogeneous version of the recurrence is:
   \[
   A_n - 3A_{n-1} = 0
   \]
   
   This can be rewritten as:
   \[
   A_n = 3A_{n-1}
   \]
   
   Assuming a solution of the form \( A_n^h = C \cdot r^n \), substituting into the homogeneous equation gives:
   \[
   C \cdot r^n = 3C \cdot r^{n-1} \\
   r = 3
   \]
   
   Thus, the homogeneous solution is:
   \[
   A_n^h = C \cdot 3^n
   \]

2. **Find a Particular Solution (\( A_n^p \)):**

   The nonhomogeneous term is constant (2). Assume \( A_n^p = K \), where \( K \) is a constant.

   Substitute into the original equation:
   \[
   K = 3K + 2 \\
   -2K = 2 \\
   K = -1
   \]
   
   Therefore, the particular solution is:
   \[
   A_n^p = -1
   \]

3. **Combine Homogeneous and Particular Solutions:**

   The general solution is:
   \[
   A_n = A_n^h + A_n^p \\
   A_n = C \cdot 3^n - 1
   \]

4. **Apply the Initial Condition to Find \( C \):**

   Using \( A_1 = 3 \):
   \[
   3 = C \cdot 3^1 - 1 \\
   3 + 1 = 3C \\
   C = \frac{4}{3}
   \]

5. **Write the Final Explicit Formula:**

   Substituting \( C = \frac{4}{3} \) into the general solution:
   \[
   A_n = \left( \frac{4}{3} \right) \cdot 3^n - 1 \\
   A_n = 4 \cdot 3^{n-1} - 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer} : 4 \times 3^{n-1} - 1}
\]

The problem involves defining an arithmetic progression (AP) where the first two terms are 4 and each subsequent term beyond the second decreases by 3. Here's how we can break it down into five conditions:

1. The first term of the sequence is 4.
2. The second term of the sequence is also 4, making the initial common difference zero between the first and second terms.
3. Starting from the third term (n > 2), each term decreases by 3 compared to the previous term.

By applying these conditions recursively, we can compute the values of subsequent terms:

- The third term (\( A_3 \)) is calculated as \( A_2 - 3 = 4 - 3 = 1 \).
- The fourth term (\( A_4 \)) is calculated as \( A_3 - 3 = 1 - 3 = -2 \).
- The fifth term (\( A_5 \)) is calculated as \( A_4 - 3 = -2 - 3 = -5 \).

Thus, the five conditions that define this sequence are:

1. \( A_1 = 4 \)
2. \( A_2 = 4 \)
3. For \( n > 2 \), \( A_n = A_{n-1} - 3 \)
4. \( A_3 = 1 \)
5. \( A_4 = -2 \)

These conditions establish a sequence where the first two terms are equal, and each subsequent term decreases by 3 from the previous one.

**Answer:**

The five conditions that define this arithmetic progression (AP) with modified common difference after n=2 are:

1. The initial term is \( A_1 = 4 \).
2. The second term is \( A_2 = 4 \), resulting in a common difference (\( d_{initial} \)) of zero.
3. For all terms beyond the second term (n > 2), each term decreases by 3: \( A_n = A_{n-1} - 3 \).
4. The third term is calculated as \( A_3 = A_2 - 3 = 1 \).
5. Following this pattern, subsequent terms can be determined recursively:
   - Fourth term: \( A_4 = A_3 - 3 = -2 \)
   - Fifth term: \( A_5 = A_4 - 3 = -5 \)

This sequence is not a standard arithmetic progression beyond the second term but follows two distinct AP rules.

The five conditions are defined as above, resulting in the values of the first five terms being 4, 4, 1, -2, and -5.

To solve the problem of finding the first few terms' sums in the given series \( 3 + \frac{6}{3} + \frac{12}{9} + \ldots \), let's break it down step by step.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Understand the Series:**
   - The given series is: \( 3 + \frac{6}{3} + \frac{12}{9} + \ldots \)
   - Let's simplify each term:
     - First term (\( n = 1 \)): \( 3 \)
     - Second term (\( n = 2 \)): \( \frac{6}{3} = 2 \)
     - Third term (\( n = 3 \)): \( \frac{12}{9} = \frac{4}{3} \)
     - And so on.

   It appears that each term can be represented as:
   \[
   a_n = \frac{6n}{3^{n-1}}
   \]
   
2. **Simplify the General Term:**
   Simplify \( a_n \):
   \[
   a_n = \frac{6n}{3^{n-1}} = 2n \cdot 3^{-(n-1)} 
   \]
   However, this might complicate finding the sum directly. Instead, let's observe that each term simplifies to \( 3 \) when evaluated:
   - For \( n = 1 \): \( a_1 = 3 \)
   - For \( n = 2 \): \( a_2 = 2 \), which is not exactly \( 3 \). It seems the series doesn't follow a constant term pattern beyond the first term. Therefore, let's re-examine the original series.

3. **Re-examining the Series:**
   Upon closer inspection:
   - The first few terms are: \( 3 + 2 + \frac{4}{3} + \ldots \)
   - It seems like each subsequent term is multiplied by \( \frac{2}{3} \):
     - Second term (\( n = 2 \)): \( 2 = 3 \times \frac{2}{3} \)
     - Third term (\( n = 3 \)): \( \frac{4}{3} = 2 \times \frac{2}{3} \)
   - Thus, this is a geometric series with the first term \( a = 3 \) and common ratio \( r = \frac{2}{3} \).

4. **Sum of the First Five Terms:**
   The formula for the sum of the first \( n \) terms of a geometric series is:
   \[
   S_n = a \times \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r}
   \]
   Plugging in the values:
   \[
   S_5 = 3 \times \frac{1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^5}{1 - \frac{2}{3}} 
   \]
   Simplify step by step:
   - Calculate \( r^5 \): \( \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^5 = \frac{32}{243} \)
   - Plug into the formula:
     \[
     S_5 = 3 \times \frac{1 - \frac{32}{243}}{\frac{1}{3}} = 3 \times \frac{\frac{211}{243}}{\frac{1}{3}} = 3 \times \frac{211}{81} = \frac{633}{81} = \frac{211}{27}
     \]
   
5. **Final Answer:**
   The sum of the first five terms is:
   \[
   \boxed{\dfrac{211}{27}}
   \]

### Summary:
The series \( 3 + \frac{6}{3} + \frac{12}{9} + \ldots \) is a geometric series with the first term \( a = 3 \) and common ratio \( r = \frac{2}{3} \). The sum of its first five terms is \( \dfrac{211}{27} \).

- **Common ratio (\(r\))**: Each term is multiplied by \(\frac{1}{2}\).
- **Last term (\(a_n\))**: \(\frac{3^{6141}}{1024}\)

### Step 2: Use the Geometric Sequence Formula
The nth term of a geometric sequence is given by:
\[
a_n = a \cdot r^{n-1}
\]
Plugging in the known values:
\[
\frac{3^{6141}}{1024} = 3 \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{n-1}
\]

\]

\]

\[
-10 = -(n - 1)
\]

\[
n - 1 = 10
\]
\[
n = 11

**Solution:**

We are given an **arithmetic sequence** where:
- The \(10^{th}\) term (\(a_{10}\)) is 384.
- The common difference (\(d\)) is 4.

Our goal is to find the \(14^{th}\) term (\(a_{14}\)) of this sequence.

**Step 1: Understanding the Arithmetic Sequence Formula**

The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term of an arithmetic sequence is:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d \]

Where:
- \(a_n\) is the \(n^{th}\) term,
- \(a_1\) is the first term,
- \(d\) is the common difference.

**Step 2: Finding the First Term (\(a_1\))**

Using the given \(10^{th}\) term (\(a_{10} = 384\)):
\[ a_{10} = a_1 + (10 - 1)d \]
\[ 384 = a_1 + 9 \times 4 \]
\[ 384 = a_1 + 36 \]

Solving for \(a_1\):
\[ a_1 = 384 - 36 \]
\[ a_1 = 348 \]

**Step 3: Finding the \(14^{th}\) Term (\(a_{14}\))**

Now, using the general formula again for \(n = 14\):
\[ a_{14} = a_1 + (14 - 1)d \]
\[ a_{14} = 348 + 13 \times 4 \]
\[ a_{14} = 348 + 52 \]
\[ a_{14} = 400 \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{400}

To determine the number of terms in a geometric progression where the first term \( a \) is equal to \( \frac{r}{4} \), and the sum of all terms equals 63, we start by using the formula for the sum of a geometric series:

\[
S_n = a \cdot \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r}
\]

Given that \( S_n = 63 \) and \( a = \frac{r}{4} \), substitute these into the equation:

\[
63 = \frac{r}{4} \cdot \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r}
\]

Multiply both sides by \( 4(1 - r) \):

\[
252(1 - r) = r(1 - r^n)
\]

Expanding and rearranging:

\[
252 - 252r = r - r^{n+1}
\]
\[
r^{n+1} - 253r + 252 = 0
\]

This equation is challenging to solve directly for \( n \) without additional constraints. Trying small integer values for \( r \):

- For \( r = 2 \), the equation becomes \( 2^{n+1} - 506 + 252 = 0 \) → \( 2^{n+1} = 254 \). This is not a power of 2.
  
- For \( r = 3 \): \( 3^{n+1} - 759 + 252 = 0 \) → \( 3^{n+1} = 507 \), which isn't a power of 3.

Testing other small values for \( n \):

***********************<think>
First, I need to identify what type of sequence or progression we are dealing with. The problem mentions "هندسی ترقی" which translates to "geometric progression." So it's definitely a geometric progression.

In a geometric progression (GP), each term is obtained by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the common ratio, denoted as \( r \).

Given:
- First term (\( a_1 \)) = 10
- Third term (\( a_3 \)) + Fifth term (\( a_5 \)) = 900

In a GP, the \( n^{th} \) term is given by:
\[ a_n = a_1 \times r^{n-1} \]

So,
\[ a_3 = 10 \times r^{2} \]
\[ a_5 = 10 \times r^{4} \]

According to the problem:
\[ 10r^2 + 10r^4 = 900 \]

Simplifying the equation:
Divide both sides by 10:
\[ r^2 + r^4 = 90 \]
\[ r^4 + r^2 - 90 = 0 \]

Let me make a substitution to simplify solving for \( r \). Let’s set \( y = r^2 \), so the equation becomes:
\[ y^2 + y - 90 = 0 \]

Now, I can solve this quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
\[ y = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \]
Where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 1 \), and \( c = -90 \).

Plugging in the values:
\[ y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 360}}{2} \]
\[ y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{361}}{2} \]
\[ y = \frac{-1 \pm 19}{2} \]

This gives two solutions:
1. \( y = \frac{18}{2} = 9 \)
2. \( y = \frac{-20}{2} = -10 \)

Since \( y = r^2 \) and squares are always non-negative, \( y = -10 \) is not a valid solution.

So,
\[ r^2 = 9 \]
Taking the square root of both sides:
\[ r = 3 \quad \text{or} \quad r = -3 \]

Therefore, the common ratio \( r \) can be either 3 or -3.
</think>

{'Final Answer': 'The common ratio is 3 or -3.'}

To determine the final amount owed for the tractor, let's break down the calculations step by step.

**Given:**
- **Principal (P):** Rs. 24,000
- **Initial Payment:** Rs. 12,000 in cash
- **Remaining Balance after Initial Payment:** \( P_{\text{remaining}} = 24,000 - 12,000 = 12,000 \) Rs.
- **Simple Interest Rate (R):** 24% per annum
- **Time Period (T):** 2 years
- **Additional Flat Fee:** Rs. 1,000

**Step 1: Calculate Simple Interest on the Remaining Balance**
\[
\text{Simple Interest (SI)} = P_{\text{remaining}} \times R \times T
\]
\[
\text{SI} = 12,000 \times 0.24 \times 2 = 5,760 \text{ Rs.}
\]

**Step 2: Calculate Total Interest**
Add the additional flat fee to the simple interest:
\[
\text{Total Interest} = \text{SI} + \text{Additional Flat Fee} = 5,760 + 1,000 = 6,760 \text{ Rs.}
\]

**Step 3: Calculate Total Amount to be Paid**
Add the total interest to the remaining balance:
\[
\text{Total Amount} = P_{\text{remaining}} + \text{Total Interest} = 12,000 + 6,760 = 18,760 \text{ Rs.}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{18760}
\]

- \( A \) is the total amount
- \( P \) is the principal amount
- \( r \) is the annual interest rate (in decimal)
- \( t \) is the time in years

To solve the problem using **Simple Interest**, we'll follow these steps:

### Given:
- **Principal (P)**: ⨉10,000
- **Rate of Interest (r)**: 5% per annum = 0.05
- **Time Periods**:
  - For 15 years
  - To find when the amount reaches ⨉20,000

### Formula for Simple Interest:
\[ A = P + (P \times r \times t) \]

Where:
- \( A \) is the total amount
- \( P \) is the principal
- \( r \) is the annual interest rate
- \( t \) is the time in years

---

### 1. Amount after **15** Years:

Plugging the values into the formula:
\[
A = 10,000 + (10,000 \times 0.05 \times 15)
\]
\[
A = 10,000 + (750 \times 15) 
\]
\[
A = 10,000 + 11,250
\]
\[
A = ⨉22,500
\]

### 2. Time to Reach ⨉20,000:

We need to find \( t \) when the total amount \( A \) becomes ⨉20,000.

Using the formula:
\[
20,000 = 10,000 + (10,000 \times 0.05 \times t)
\]
Subtracting the principal from both sides:
\[
10,000 = 500t
\]
Dividing both sides by 500:
\[
t = \frac{10,000}{500} = 20 \text{ years}
\]

---

### Final Answers:

- **Amount after 15 years**: ⨉22,500
- **Time to reach ⨉20,000**: 20 years

Given the problem, we need to determine the original duration of a project based on changes in the number of workers. 

We know that:
- On day 1, there were 150 workers.
- On day 2, there were 146 workers (a reduction of 4).
- From day 3 onwards, each subsequent day had 4 more workers than the previous day.

The total work done must remain the same as originally planned. Let \( D \) be the original number of days.

The total man-days for the project is calculated as follows:
- Day 1: 150 workers
- Day 2: 146 workers
- From day 3 to day \( D \): Each day's worker count forms an arithmetic sequence starting at 150 with a common difference of 4.

The sum of the series from day 3 to day \( D \) is calculated using the formula for the sum of an arithmetic series:
\[ S' = \frac{(D - 2)}{2} \times [2 \times 150 + (D - 3) \times 4] \]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[ S' = \frac{(D - 2)(4D + 288)}{2} \]

Adding the workers from day 1 and day 2:
\[ S = 150 + 146 + \frac{(D - 2)(4D + 288)}{2} \]
Simplifying further:
\[ S = 296 + \frac{(D - 2)(4D + 288)}{2} \]
\[ S = 296 + (2D^2 + 140D - 288) \]
\[ S = 2D^2 + 140D + 8 \]

Setting this equal to the original work \( W = 150D \):
\[ 2D^2 + 140D + 8 = 150D \]
Simplifying:
\[ 2D^2 - 10D + 8 = 0 \]
Dividing by 2:
\[ D^2 - 5D + 4 = 0 \]
Factoring:
\[ (D - 1)(D - 4) = 0 \]

Thus, \( D = 1 \) or \( D = 4 \). Since \( D \) must be more than 2, we have \( D = 4 \).

Therefore, the original duration of the project was \(\boxed{4}\) days.

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. I'm trying to find the 12th term of an arithmetic progression (AP) where the first few terms are given as 4, 9, 14,...

First, I need to recall what defines an arithmetic progression. In an AP, each term after the first is obtained by adding a constant difference to the preceding term. This constant is called the common difference.

Looking at the given terms:
- First term (a₁) = 4
- Second term (a₂) = 9
- Third term (a₃) = 14

To find the common difference (d), I can subtract the first term from the second term:
\[ d = a₂ - a₁ = 9 - 4 = 5 \]

Now that I have the common difference, I can use the formula for the nth term of an AP to find the 12th term. The formula is:
\[ a_n = a₁ + (n - 1)d \]

Plugging in the values for n = 12, a₁ = 4, and d = 5:
\[
a_{12} = 4 + (12 - 1) \times 5 \\
a_{12} = 4 + 11 \times 5 \\
a_{12} = 4 + 55 \\
a_{12} = 59
\]

So, the 12th term of this AP is **59**.
</think>

To find the 12th term of the arithmetic progression (AP) with the given terms 4, 9, 14,... follow these steps:

1. **Identify the First Term and Common Difference:**
   - First term (\(a_1\)) = 4
   - Second term (\(a_2\)) = 9
   - Third term (\(a_3\)) = 14

2. **Calculate the Common Difference (\(d\)):**
   \[
   d = a_2 - a_1 = 9 - 4 = 5
   \]

3. **Use the Formula for the \(n^{th}\) Term of an AP:**
   The formula is:
   \[
   a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d
   \]
   
4. **Find the 12th Term (\(a_{12}\)):**
   Substitute \(n = 12\), \(a_1 = 4\), and \(d = 5\) into the formula:
   \[
   a_{12} = 4 + (12 - 1) \times 5 \\
   a_{12} = 4 + 11 \times 5 \\
   a_{12} = 4 + 55 \\
   a_{12} = 59
   \]

\[
\boxed{59}

### Given:
- The initial terms are: **5, 11, 17, 23, ...**
  
We need to check if **330** is part of this sequence. 

### Step-by-Step Analysis:

1. **Identify the Pattern:**
   - Let's first understand how these numbers are generated.
   - Calculate the differences between consecutive terms:
     - \( 11 - 5 = 6 \)
     - \( 17 - 11 = 6 \)
     - \( 23 - 17 = 6 \)
   - The common difference is **6**, indicating an Arithmetic Progression (AP).

2. **General Form of AP:**
   - The nth term of an AP can be expressed as:
     \[
     a_n = a_1 + (n-1)d
     \]
     where:
     - \( a_1 = 5 \) (first term)
     - \( d = 6 \) (common difference)

3. **Find if 330 is in the Sequence:**
   - Set up the equation to find \( n \):
     \[
     330 = 5 + (n-1) \times 6
     \]
   - Simplify:
     \[
     330 - 5 = (n-1) \times 6 \\
     325 = 6(n - 1) \\
     n - 1 = \frac{325}{6} \\
     n - 1 ≈ 54.1667
     \]
   - Since \( n \) must be an integer, **330** is not a term in this sequence.

Based on the analysis, **330** does not appear in the given list following the Arithmetic Mean (AM) pattern.

\[

\]

- \( a_n \) is the nth term,
- \( a_1 \) is the first term,
- \( d \) is the common difference,
- \( n \) is the number of terms.

\[

- **Last term (\(a_n\))**: 4
- **Common difference (\(d\))**: -2

We use the formula for the nth term of an AP:
\[
a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d
\]

Plugging in the known values:
\[
4 = 46 + (n - 1)(-2)
\]

Simplify the equation step by step:

\[
4 = 46 - 2(n - 1)
\]
\[
4 = 46 - 2n + 2
\]
\[
4 = 48 - 2n
\]
\[
2n = 48 - 4
\]
\[
2n = 44
\]
\[
n = \frac{44}{2} = 22
\]

Thus, there are **22 rows** of flower plants in the bed.

I need to find the 58th term of a geometric progression where the 6th term is 24 and the last term is 212.

First, I recall that in a GP, each term is found by multiplying the previous term by a common ratio (r). The general formula for the nth term is:

a_n = a_1 * r^(n-1)

Given:
6th term (a_6) = 24
Last term (a_l) = 212

I can set up two equations using these terms to solve for r and a_1.

Dividing the equation for the last term by the one for the 6th term will eliminate a_1, allowing me to solve for r.
</think>

To find the 58th term of the geometric progression (GP), let's follow these steps:

### **Given:**
- The 6th term (\(a_6\)) = 24
- The last term (\(a_l\)) = 212

### **Solution:**

In a GP, each term can be found using the formula:
\[ a_n = a_1 \times r^{n-1} \]
where:
- \(a_n\) is the nth term,
- \(a_1\) is the first term,
- \(r\) is the common ratio.

However, since we are given two specific terms (\(a_6\) and \(a_l\)), we can set up a system of equations to find \(r\) (the common ratio) without needing \(a_1\).

**Step 1:** Set up the equations based on the given terms.
\[ a_6 = a_1 \times r^{5} = 24 \]
\[ a_l = a_1 \times r^{l-1} = 212 \]

Since we don't know \(a_1\) or \(r\), and we're asked for the 58th term, let's assume that the last term given is after the 6th term. To simplify, if \(l - 6 = 52\), meaning there are 52 terms between the 6th and the last term.

**Step 2:** Divide the two equations to eliminate \(a_1\).
\[ \frac{a_l}{a_6} = \frac{a_1 \times r^{l-1}}{a_1 \times r^{5}} = r^{(l - 6)} \]
Assuming there are 52 terms between the 6th and last term:
\[ \frac{212}{24} = r^{52} \]

**Step 3:** Solve for \(r\).
\[ r^{52} = \frac{212}{24} \approx 8.8333 \]
Taking the 52nd root:
\[ r \approx (8.8333)^{\frac{1}{52}} \approx e^{\ln(8.8333)/52} \approx e^{0.1465} \approx 1.1587 \]

**Step 4:** Now, find the first term \(a_1\) using \(a_6\):
\[ a_6 = a_1 \times r^{5} = 24 \]
\[ a_1 = \frac{24}{r^{5}} \approx \frac{24}{(1.1587)^{5}} \approx \frac{24}{1.932} \approx 12.446 \]

**Step 5:** Finally, find the 58th term (\(a_{58}\)):
\[ a_{58} = a_1 \times r^{57} \]
Since we calculated \(r\) based on 52 terms from the 6th to the last term (which is term 58), and assuming that:
\[ r^{52} = \frac{212}{24} \]
We can express \(r^{57}\) as:
\[ r^{57} = r^{(52 + 5)} = r^{52} \times r^{5} = \left(\frac{212}{24}\right) \times (1.1587)^5 \approx 8.8333 \times 1.932 \approx 17.067 \]

Therefore:
\[ a_{58} = 12.446 \times 17.067 \approx 212 \]

However, since the last term is already given as 212 for term \(l\), and assuming \(l = 58\), we can conclude:

### **Final Answer:**
\[ \boxed{212} \]

1. **Calculate Total Interest Needed:**
   
   \[
   \text{Total Interest} = \text{Total Amount Desired} - \text{Principal} = 14,000 - 10,000 = 4,000
   \]

2. **Determine the Number of Years Required:**

   Since Rs. 400 is paid annually towards interest:

   \[
   \text{Number of Years} = \frac{\text{Total Interest}}{\text{Annual Interest Payment}} = \frac{4,000}{400} = 10 \text{ years}

   \[

   \]

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We're given an arithmetic progression (AP) where the common difference \( D = 14 \), and we need to find the sum of the first 44 terms when the 44th term is 298.

First, recall that in an AP, each term after the first is obtained by adding a constant difference to the preceding term. The general formula for the \( n \)-th term (\( T_n \)) of an AP is:

\[ T_n = a + (n - 1)D \]

Here:
- \( T_n \) is the \( n \)-th term,
- \( a \) is the first term,
- \( D \) is the common difference,
- \( n \) is the number of terms.

We're given that the 44th term (\( T_{44} \)) is 298, and \( D = 14 \). Plugging these values into the formula:

\[ 298 = a + (44 - 1)(14) \]

Let's compute \( (44 - 1)(14) \):

\[ 43 \times 14 = 602 \]

So, the equation becomes:

\[ 298 = a + 602 \]

Now, solving for \( a \):

\[ a = 298 - 602 \]
\[ a = -304 \]

The first term (\( a \)) is -304.

Next, we need to find the sum of the first 44 terms. The formula for the sum (\( S_n \)) of the first \( n \) terms of an AP is:

\[ S_n = \frac{n}{2} (a + T_n) \]

Plugging in the known values:
- \( n = 44 \),
- \( a = -304 \),
- \( T_{44} = 298 \),

So,

\[ S_{44} = \frac{44}{2} (-304 + 298) \]

First, calculate the sum inside the parentheses:

\[ -304 + 298 = -6 \]

Then multiply by \( \frac{44}{2} \):

\[ S_{44} = 22 \times (-6) \]
\[ S_{44} = -132 \]

Therefore, the sum of the first 44 terms is -132.

To determine the **common difference** (\( d \)) of an arithmetic progression where the 20th term exceeds the average over 34 days by 14 units, follow these steps:

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Understanding the Problem:**
   - Let’s denote:
     - The first term as \( a \).
     - The common difference as \( d \).
     - The number of terms = 34.
     - The average over 34 days is calculated by dividing the sum of all terms by 34.

2. **Expressing the Sum and Average:**
   - **Sum of the first \( n \) terms (\( S_n \)) in an arithmetic progression:**
     \[
     S_{34} = \frac{34}{2}[2a + (34 - 1)d] = 17[2a + 33d]
     \]
   - **Average over 34 days (\( A \)):**
     \[
     A = \frac{S_{34}}{34} = \frac{17[2a + 33d]}{34} = \frac{2a + 33d}{2}
     \]

3. **Expressing the 20th Term:**
   - The \( n \)-th term of an arithmetic progression:
     \[
     T_{20} = a + (20 - 1)d = a + 19d
     \]
   
4. **Setting Up the Given Condition:**
   - It’s given that the 20th term exceeds the average by 14 units:
     \[
     T_{20} = A + 14
     \]
   - Substituting \( T_{20} \) and \( A \):
     \[
     a + 19d = \frac{2a + 33d}{2} + 14
     \]

     \[
     2(a + 19d) = 2a + 33d + 28
     \]
     \[
     2a + 38d = 2a + 33d + 28
     \]

     5d = 28
     \]

     \]

\boxed{5.6}

\]
\[
ab = 1260
\]

This can be represented as the quadratic equation:
\[
x^2 - (a + b)x + ab = 0 \\
x^2 - 12x + 1260 = 0
\]

To solve for \(x\), I'll use the quadratic formula:
\[
x = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{(12)^2 - 4 \times 1 \times 1260}}{2} \\
x = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{144 - 5040}}{2} \\
x = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{-4896}}{2}
\]

Since the discriminant is negative, there are no real solutions. This means that such numbers \(a\) and \(b\) do not exist within the realm of real numbers.
</think>

To solve the problem where two numbers have a sum of 12 and their geometric mean is also given as \(6 \sqrt{35}\), we can follow these steps:

Let's denote the two numbers by \(a\) and \(b\).

**Given:**
1. The sum of the numbers:
   \[
   a + b = 12
   \]
   
2. Their geometric mean is \(6 \sqrt{35}\):
   \[
   \sqrt{ab} = 6 \sqrt{35}
   \]

**Step 1: Find the product of the numbers**

Square both sides of the geometric mean equation to find \(ab\):
\[
(\sqrt{ab})^2 = (6 \sqrt{35})^2 \\
ab = 36 \times 35 \\
ab = 1260
\]

**Step 2: Form a quadratic equation**

Using the sum and product of the numbers, we can form the quadratic equation:
\[
x^2 - (a + b)x + ab = 0 \\
x^2 - 12x + 1260 = 0
\]

**Step 3: Solve the quadratic equation**

Use the quadratic formula to find the roots:
\[
x = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{(12)^2 - 4 \times 1 \times 1260}}{2} \\
x = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{144 - 5040}}{2} \\
x = \frac{12 \pm \sqrt{-4896}}{2}
\]

Since the discriminant (\(\sqrt{-4896}\)) is negative, there are no real solutions for \(a\) and \(b\).

**Conclusion:**

There are **no real numbers** that satisfy both conditions of having a sum of 12 and a geometric mean of \(6 \sqrt{35}\). Therefore, such numbers do not exist within the realm of real numbers.

First, I need to understand that calculating the average growth rate typically involves using the geometric mean because it accounts for compounding effects over multiple periods.

I have the annual percentage gains from 2000 to 2005, which are 50%, 72%, 54%, 82%, and 93%. To find the geometric mean, I'll follow these steps:

1. Convert each percentage gain into a decimal form by adding 1 to each value.
   
   - 50% becomes 1.5
   - 72% becomes 1.72
   - 54% becomes 1.54
   - 82% becomes 1.82
   - 93% becomes 1.93

2. Multiply all these decimal values together to get the product.

   Product = 1.5 * 1.72 * 1.54 * 1.82 * 1.93 ≈ 7.4647

3. Take the nth root of the product, where n is the number of periods (in this case, 5).

   Fifth Root = 7.4647^(1/5) ≈ 1.4482

4. Subtract 1 from this result to convert it back to a percentage.

   Geometric Mean Growth Rate ≈ 0.4482 or 44.82%

This growth rate indicates that the business's profits grew by approximately 44.82% on average each year over the five-year period.
</think>

**Solution:**

To determine the **average annual growth rate (geometric mean)** of the business profits from 2000 to 2005, follow these steps:

1. **List of Annual Percentage Gains:**
   - 2000-2001: 50% → Decimal Form: \(1 + \frac{50}{100} = 1.5\)
   - 2001-2002: 72% → Decimal Form: \(1 + \frac{72}{100} = 1.72\)
   - 2002-2003: 54% → Decimal Form: \(1 + \frac{54}{100} = 1.54\)
   - 2003-2004: 82% → Decimal Form: \(1 + \frac{82}{100} = 1.82\)
   - 2004-2005: 93% → Decimal Form: \(1 + \frac{93}{100} = 1.93\)

2. **Calculate the Product of All Decimal Values:**
   
   \[
   \text{Product} = 1.5 \times 1.72 \times 1.54 \times 1.82 \times 1.93
   \]
   
   Calculating this:
   
   \[
   \text{Product} \approx 7.4647
   \]

3. **Determine the Number of Periods:**
   
   There are **5** periods (from 2000 to 2005).

4. **Compute the Geometric Mean:**
   
   The geometric mean is calculated by taking the nth root of the product, where \( n \) is the number of periods.
   
   \[
   \text{Geometric Mean} = \sqrt[5]{7.4647}
   \]
   
   Calculating this:
   
   \[
   \text{Geometric Mean} \approx 1.4482
   \]

5. **Convert Back to Percentage:**
   
   Subtract 1 from the geometric mean and multiply by 100%.
   
   \[
   \text{Average Growth Rate} = (1.4482 - 1) \times 100\% = 0.4482 \times 100\% = 44.82\%
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The average annual growth rate is:

The problem involves calculating the average growth rate of a population over three years, considering two years of growth and one year of decline.

First Year:
1.15 represents a 15% increase in the first year.
Second Year:
(1.15)^2 accounts for another 15% increase on the already increased population from the first year.
Third Year:
The population decreases by 5%, which is equivalent to multiplying by 0.95.

To find the average growth rate, we need to calculate the geometric mean of these three annual growth factors: two years of 1.15 and one year of 0.95.

The formula for the geometric mean (G) is:

G = (1.15 * 1.15 * 0.95)^(1/3)

Calculating this will give us the average growth rate per annum that, when applied each year, results in the same final population after three years.
</think>

To determine the **average annual growth rate** over three years considering a 15% increase for two years and a subsequent 5% decrease in the third year, we can use the concept of the geometric mean.

### **Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Growth Factors:**
   - **First Year (Year 1):** \( 1 + 0.15 = 1.15 \)
   - **Second Year (Year 2):** \( 1 + 0.15 = 1.15 \) *(Another increase of 15%)*
   - **Third Year (Year 3):** \( 1 - 0.05 = 0.95 \) *(A decrease of 5%)*

2. **Calculate the Geometric Mean:**
   
   The geometric mean (\( G \)) is used to find a single growth rate that would result in the same final value over multiple periods.

   \[
   G = \sqrt[3]{1.15 \times 1.15 \times 0.95}
   \]

3. **Compute the Product:**
   
   \[
   1.15 \times 1.15 \times 0.95 = (1.15)^2 \times 0.95
   \]
   
   First, calculate \( (1.15)^2 \):
   
   \[
   1.15 \times 1.15 = 1.3225
   \]
   
   Then multiply by 0.95:
   
   \[
   1.3225 \times 0.95 = 1.256375
   \]

4. **Find the Cube Root:**
   
   Now, find the cube root of \( 1.256375 \):
   
   \[
   G = \sqrt[3]{1.256375} \approx 1.078
   \]
   
   This means the average annual growth rate is approximately **7.8%**.

### **Final Answer:**

\boxed{\text{The average annual growth rate over three years is approximately } 7.8\%.}

The original dataset consists of 14 numbers with an arithmetic mean (AM) of 6. Therefore, the total sum is \(14 \times 6 = 84\).

When each number is included thrice, we effectively have three copies of each original number in the new dataset. This means the count of numbers increases from 14 to \(14 \times 3 = 42\). 

The total sum of the new dataset becomes \(84 \times 3 = 252\).

To find the new arithmetic mean (AM) of the expanded dataset, we divide the new total sum by the new count:
\[
\text{New AM} = \frac{252}{42} = 6
\]

Thus, including each number thrice does not change the arithmetic mean; it remains at 6.
</think>

The problem involves calculating how the arithmetic mean (AM) changes when each of the original numbers is included three times.

**Solution:**

1. **Original Dataset:**
   - Number of terms (\(n\)) = 14
   - Arithmetic Mean (\(AM\)) = 6
   - Total sum = \( n \times AM = 14 \times 6 = 84 \)

2. **Including Each Number Thrice:**
   - Each number is included three times, so the new count of numbers becomes \(14 \times 3 = 42\).
   - The total sum becomes \(84 \times 3 = 252\) (since each original number contributes three times its value to the sum).

3. **New Arithmetic Mean:**
   - New Total Sum = 252
   - New Number of Terms = 42
   - New AM = \( \frac{252}{42} = 6 \)

**Final Answer:**  
The new arithmetic mean remains 6.
